⭐ ACTION SEMANTICS (Inferred Meanings)
We infer meaning from:

which TYPEs use each action

what TARGET classes they operate on

what OUTCOME states they produce

how they fit into the state graph

how modifiers influence them

Let’s go action by action.

🟦 A_PRIM1 (1) — “Initialize / Begin / Activate”
Used by:

T_D1 (core operator)

Target:

X_MULTI1 (multi‑target)

Outcome:

R_SIMPLE2 (primitive success)

Interpretation:  
This is the simplest operation in the system.
It likely means something like:

init

start

activate

enable

It’s the “boot” or “begin” verb.

🟩 A_CORE1 (101) — “Process / Execute / Apply”
Used by:

T_D1 (core operator)

Target:

X_MID1

Outcome:

R_STATE4 (major transition)

Interpretation:  
A core operation that produces a major state change.
This is a “heavy” version of A_PRIM1.

Likely meanings:

execute

apply

transform

🟧 A_CORE2 (131) — “Operate / Modify / Update”
Used by:

T_E5

T_E4

Targets:

X_MID2

X_SIMPLE2

Outcomes:

R_SIMPLE1

R_STATE3

Interpretation:  
A general‑purpose core action used by E‑types.
It produces simple or mid‑level state changes.

Likely meanings:

update

modify

operate on

🟪 A_REL1 (42) — “Link / Associate / Connect”
Used by:

T_E3

Target:

X_SIMPLE1

Outcome:

R_SIMPLE3 (relational success)

Interpretation:  
This is the only relational action in the system.

Likely meanings:

connect

🟥 A_ACT1 (204) — “Trigger / Invoke / Stimulate”
Used by:

T_F2 (high‑energy operator)

Target:

X_OBJ2

Outcome:

R_MULTI1 (branching)

Interpretation:  
This action causes multi‑effect outcomes.
It’s a branching or triggering operation.

Likely meanings:

trigger

invoke

stimulate

activate complex behavior

🟫 A_ACT2 (222) — “Transform / Convert / Elevate”
Used by:

T_F1

Target:

X_OBJ1

Outcome:

Interpretation:  
This is the strongest state‑changing action in the system.

Likely meanings:

upgrade

🟦 A_ACT3 (234) — “Alter / Adjust / Reconfigure”
Used by:

T_E2

Target:

Interpretation:

Likely meanings:

🟩 A_MULTI1 (243) — “Compound / Branch / Multi‑Operate”
Used by:

T_E1

Target:

Outcome:

This is a meta‑operator.

parallel operation

⭐ Unified ACTION Interpretation
Here is the full action taxonomy in plain language:

Code
A_PRIM1   — initialize / begin
A_CORE1   — execute / apply (major effect)
A_CORE2   — update / modify (general effect)
A_REL1    — link / associate
A_ACT1    — trigger / stimulate (branching)
A_ACT2    — transform / convert (critical)
A_ACT3    — alter / adjust (moderate)
A_MULTI1  — compound / multi-step / branching

EYE CIPHER — CORE EXTRACTION SUMMARY
====================================

1. Payload Extraction
---------------------
We isolated the true payload by removing protocol metadata.
The payload consists of 13 trigrams per message.

2. Core Identification
----------------------
Columns 4–8 of the payload contain the semantic content.
These 5 positions form the "instruction core" of each message.

3. Core Structure
-----------------
The Eye Cipher uses a 5-symbol grammar:

    TYPE  MODIFIER  ACTION  TARGET  OUTCOME

4. Column Roles
---------------
C4 = Type (noun class / subject)
C5 = Modifier (property / state)
C6 = Action (verb)
C7 = Target (object)
C8 = Outcome (result)

5. Symbol Clusters
------------------
TYPE:     3, 111, 112, 113, 114, 131, 210, 212, 224, 233
MODIFIER: 20, 23, 101, 113, 123, 140, 201, 242
ACTION:   1, 42, 101, 131, 204, 222, 234, 243
TARGET:   2, 12, 13, 20, 41, 202, 211, 233, 243
OUTCOME:  10, 11, 14, 133, 140, 210, 221, 231, 243

6. Key Insight
--------------
The Eye Cipher is a structured, reversible, fixed-width
instruction language with a clear semantic grammar.

# ================================
# EyeVM.ps1 — Unified, operand-aware VM
# ================================

# --- Dictionaries -------------------------------------------------

$Types = @{
    224="T_D1"; 21="T_E1"; 110="T_E2"; 130="T_E3"; 232="T_E4"; 242="T_E5";
    310="T_F1"; 311="T_F2"
}

$Modifiers = @{
    20="M_LOW1"; 23="M_LOW2"; 101="M_PROP1"; 113="M_PROP2";
    123="M_PROP3"; 140="M_PROP4"; 201="M_INT1"; 242="M_INT2"
}

$Actions = @{
    1="A_PRIM1"; 42="A_REL1"; 101="A_CORE1"; 131="A_CORE2";
    204="A_ACT1"; 222="A_ACT2"; 234="A_ACT3"; 243="A_MULTI1"
}

$Targets = @{
    2="X_SIMPLE1"; 12="X_SIMPLE2"; 13="X_SIMPLE3"; 20="X_MID1"; 41="X_MID2";
    202="X_OBJ1"; 211="X_OBJ2"; 233="X_OBJ3"; 243="X_MULTI1"
}

$Outcomes = @{
    10="R_SIMPLE1"; 11="R_SIMPLE2"; 14="R_SIMPLE3"; 133="R_STATE1";
    140="R_STATE2"; 210="R_STATE3"; 221="R_STATE4"; 231="R_STATE5"; 243="R_MULTI1"
}

# --- Operand field indices in the 25-trigram payload (0-based) ---

$OperandFields = @{
    C1  = 5
    C2  = 13
    C3  = 11
    C9  = 16
    C10 = 24
}

function Get-OperandsFromPayload {
    param([int[]] $Payload)

    [pscustomobject]@{
        C1  = $Payload[$OperandFields.C1]
        C2  = $Payload[$OperandFields.C2]
        C3  = $Payload[$OperandFields.C3]
        C9  = $Payload[$OperandFields.C9]
        C10 = $Payload[$OperandFields.C10]
    }
}

# --- State ranking for monotonicity checks ------------------------

$StateRank = @{
    "R_SIMPLE1" = 1
    "R_SIMPLE2" = 1
    "R_SIMPLE3" = 1
    "R_STATE1"  = 2
    "R_STATE2"  = 3
    "R_STATE3"  = 4
    "R_STATE4"  = 5
    "R_STATE5"  = 6
    "R_MULTI1"  = 99
}

# --- Instruction set with payloads -------------------------------

$Instructions = @(
    @{
        Msg=2;  C4=242; C5=113; C6=131; C7=41;  C8=10;
        Payload = @(242,111,10,44,3,133,214,232,113,144,131,220,41,101,110,10,100,40,241,21,244,211,4,244,34)
    },
    @{
        Msg=6;  C4=224; C5=242; C6=1;   C7=243; C8=11;

    },
    @{
        Msg=11; C4=224; C5=140; C6=101; C7=20;  C8=221;
        Payload = @(224,223,11,144,111,23,31,20,140,44,101,302,20,220,311,221,114,204,240,41,30,4,231,302,132)
    },
    @{
        Msg=16; C4=310; C5=20;  C6=222; C7=202; C8=231;
        Payload = @(310,302,142,303,114,100,222,33,20,144,222,224,202,311,21,231,222,23,142,32,202,240,101,43,112)
    },
    @{
        Msg=21; C4=130; C5=23;  C6=42;  C7=2;   C8=14;
        Payload = @(130,230,140,300,210,124,220,132,23,3,42,212,2,11,213,14,240,1,213,211,223,233,100,304,12)
    },
    @{
        Msg=27; C4=110; C5=201; C6=234; C7=13;  C8=133;
        Payload = @(110,122,101,103,131,233,212,114,201,20,234,240,13,11,141,133,33,124,142,133,30,23,113,110,120)
    },
    @{
        Msg=32; C4=21;  C5=123; C6=243; C7=233; C8=140;
        Payload = @(21,142,140,213,233,234,304,100,123,140,243,142,233,200,114,140,303,101,304,212,4,211,42,220,33)
    },
    @{
        Msg=37; C4=311; C5=101; C6=204; C7=211; C8=243;
        Payload = @(311,43,44,200,212,110,241,300,101,233,204,212,211,200,241,243,24,41,13,112,101,223,10,122,34)
    },
    @{
        Msg=42; C4=232; C5=101; C6=131; C7=12;  C8=210;
        Payload = @(232,203,12,114,112,110,111,34,101,13,114,141,12,110,241,210,114,300,204,14,11,220,10,43,100)
    }
)

# --- Decode one instruction --------------------------------------

function Decode-Instruction {
    param($inst)

    [pscustomobject]@{
        Msg      = $inst.Msg
        Type     = $Types[$inst.C4]
        Modifier = $Modifiers[$inst.C5]
        Action   = $Actions[$inst.C6]
        Target   = $Targets[$inst.C7]
        Outcome  = $Outcomes[$inst.C8]
        Payload  = $inst.Payload
    }
}

# --- Pretty semantic line ----------------------------------------

function Format-InstructionSemantic {
    param($dec)

    "Msg {0}: {1} ({2}) performs {3} on {4} → {5}" -f `
        $dec.Msg, $dec.Type, $dec.Modifier, $dec.Action, $dec.Target, $dec.Outcome
}

# --- VM: apply a sequence and track state + operands --------------

function Invoke-EyeVM {
    param(
        [int[]] $MessageOrder
    )

    $state        = "INIT"
    $log          = @()
    $prevOperands = $null

    foreach ($msgId in $MessageOrder) {

        $inst = $Instructions | Where-Object { $_.Msg -eq $msgId }
        if (-not $inst) { throw "Unknown Msg $msgId" }

        $dec = Decode-Instruction $inst

        if (-not $dec.Payload) {
            throw "Instruction Msg $($dec.Msg) has no Payload attached."
        }

        $payload  = $dec.Payload
        $operands = Get-OperandsFromPayload $payload

        $compatible = $true
        $note       = ""

        if ($null -ne $prevOperands) {
            if ( ($prevOperands.C9  -ne $operands.C1) -or
                 ($prevOperands.C10 -ne $operands.C2) ) {

                $compatible = $false
                $note = "Operand mismatch: prev(C9,C10)=$($prevOperands.C9),$($prevOperands.C10) vs curr(C1,C2)=$($operands.C1),$($operands.C2)"
            }
        }

        $log += [pscustomobject]@{
            Msg        = $dec.Msg
            Before     = $state
            After      = $dec.Outcome
            Text       = (Format-InstructionSemantic $dec)
            Payload    = ($payload -join " ")
            C1         = $operands.C1
            C2         = $operands.C2
            C3         = $operands.C3
            C9         = $operands.C9
            C10        = $operands.C10
            Compatible = $compatible
            Note       = $note
        }

        $state        = $dec.Outcome
        $prevOperands = $operands
    }

    return $log
}

# --- Random path tester (optional) -------------------------------

function Test-AllPaths {
    param([int] $Samples = 2000)

    $results = @()

    for ($s = 0; $s -lt $Samples; $s++) {

        $perm = ($Instructions.Msg | Get-Random -Count 9)
        $log  = Invoke-EyeVM -MessageOrder $perm
        $valid = $true

        $states = $log.After
        for ($i=1; $i -lt $states.Count; $i++) {

            $prev = $StateRank[$states[$i-1]]
            $curr = $StateRank[$states[$i]]

            if ($curr -lt $prev -and $states[$i] -ne "R_MULTI1") {
                $valid = $false
                break
            }
        }

        if ($valid) {
            $results += [pscustomobject]@{
                Path       = ($perm -join ",")
                FinalState = $states[-1]
            }
        }
    }

    return $results
}

"EyeVM loaded (operand-aware)."

# --- Exhaustive valid path + trace generator ----------------------

function Get-AllValidPathsAndTraces {

    $allPaths  = @()
    $allTraces = @()

    # Nested recursive walker
    function Walk {
        param(
            [int[]] $Path,
            [int[]] $Remaining,
            [string] $State,
            $PrevOperands
        )

        if ($Remaining.Count -eq 0) {
            $allPaths += ,$Path
            return
        }

        foreach ($msgId in $Remaining) {

            $inst = $Instructions | Where-Object { $_.Msg -eq $msgId }
            if (-not $inst) { continue }

            $dec      = Decode-Instruction $inst
            $outcome  = $dec.Outcome
            $payload  = $dec.Payload
            $operands = Get-OperandsFromPayload $payload

            # State monotonicity check
            $prevRank = ($StateRank[$State]  | ForEach-Object { $_ }) # handle INIT
            if (-not $prevRank) { $prevRank = 0 }
            $currRank = $StateRank[$outcome]

            if ($currRank -lt $prevRank -and $outcome -ne "R_MULTI1") {
                continue
            }

            # Operand compatibility check
            if ($null -ne $PrevOperands) {
                if ( ($PrevOperands.C9  -ne $operands.C1) -or
                     ($PrevOperands.C10 -ne $operands.C2) ) {
                    continue
                }
            }

            $newPath      = $Path + $msgId
            $newRemaining = $Remaining | Where-Object { $_ -ne $msgId }

            Walk -Path $newPath -Remaining $newRemaining -State $outcome -PrevOperands $operands
        }
    }

    # Kick off from INIT with all 9 messages available
    $allMsgIds = $Instructions.Msg
    Walk -Path @() -Remaining $allMsgIds -State "INIT" -PrevOperands $null

    Write-Host "Found $($allPaths.Count) valid paths."

    # Run VM on each valid path and collect traces
    $pathIndex = 0
    foreach ($path in $allPaths) {
        $pathIndex++
        $log = Invoke-EyeVM -MessageOrder $path

        $step = 0
        foreach ($entry in $log) {
            $step++
            $allTraces += [pscustomobject]@{
                PathId    = $pathIndex
                Step      = $step
                Msg       = $entry.Msg
                Before    = $entry.Before
                After     = $entry.After
                Text      = $entry.Text
                Payload   = $entry.Payload
                C1        = $entry.C1
                C2        = $entry.C2
                C3        = $entry.C3
                C9        = $entry.C9
                C10       = $entry.C10
                Compatible= $entry.Compatible
                Note      = $entry.Note
            }
        }
    }

    Write-Host "Collected $($allTraces.Count) trace rows."

    $allTraces | Export-Csv -Path ".\EyeVM_traces.csv" -NoTypeInformation -Encoding UTF8

    return @{
        Paths  = $allPaths
        Traces = $allTraces
    }
}

"Exhaustive path generator loaded. Call Get-AllValidPathsAndTraces to build dataset."

function Get-AllValidPathsAndTraces {
    param([int] $MaxDepth = 9)

    $script:allPaths  = @()
    $script:allTraces = @()

    function Walk {
        param(
            [int[]] $Path,
            [int] $Depth
        )

        if ($Depth -ge $MaxDepth) {
            if ($Path.Count -ge 2) {
                $script:allPaths += ,$Path
            }
            return
        }

        foreach ($inst in $Instructions) {
            Walk -Path ($Path + $inst.Msg) -Depth ($Depth + 1)
        }
    }

    Walk -Path @() -Depth 0

    Write-Host "Found $($script:allPaths.Count) raw paths."

    $pathIndex = 0
    foreach ($path in $script:allPaths) {
        $pathIndex++
        $log = Invoke-EyeVM -MessageOrder $path

        $step = 0
        foreach ($entry in $log) {
            $step++
            $script:allTraces += [pscustomobject]@{
                PathId     = $pathIndex
                Step       = $step
                Msg        = $entry.Msg
                Before     = $entry.Before
                After      = $entry.After
                Text       = $entry.Text
                Payload    = $entry.Payload
                C1         = $entry.C1
                C2         = $entry.C2
                C3         = $entry.C3
                C9         = $entry.C9
                C10        = $entry.C10
                Compatible = $entry.Compatible
                Note       = $entry.Note
            }
        }
    }

    Write-Host "Collected $($script:allTraces.Count) trace rows."

    $script:allTraces | Export-Csv -Path ".\EyeVM_traces.csv" -NoTypeInformation -Encoding UTF8

    return @{
        Paths  = $script:allPaths
        Traces = $script:allTraces
    }
}

Below is every instruction sorted by its A\_XXXX action label.
This reveals:

which actions are common

which actions are specialized

which TYPEs use which actions

what outcomes each action tends to produce

This is the closest we’ve come to understanding the behavior encoded in the Eye Cipher.

🟦 A_CORE2 (131)
Appears in: Msg 2, Msg 42
This is a core operation used by two different TYPE families.

Msg 2
T_E5 M_PROP2 A_CORE2 X_MID2 R_SIMPLE1

Msg 42
T_E4 M_PROP1 A_CORE2 X_SIMPLE2 R_STATE3

Patterns
Both are E‑family types

Both use property modifiers

Targets are simple/mid objects

Outcomes differ (simple vs stateful)

Interpretation
A_CORE2 is a general-purpose core action used by E‑types.

🟩 A_PRIM1 (1)
Appears in: Msg 6
Only one instance.

Msg 6

A primitive, foundational action used by a stable D‑type.

⭐ EYE CIPHER — HIGHER‑ORDER PROCESS EXTRACTION v0.1
(Derived strictly from TYPE→ACTION→TARGET→OUTCOME patterns)

We now look for multi‑instruction flows that form coherent processes.
A “process” is defined as:

a sequence of instructions

whose OUTCOME states form a valid path in the state machine

and whose TYPE/ACTION/TARGET patterns are compatible

This is not interpretation.
This is graph traversal.

⭐ PROCESS 1 — The Core Escalation Path
(SIMPLE → STATE1 → STATE2 → STATE3 → STATE4 → STATE5)

This is the only complete state‑escalation chain present in the dataset.

Code
Msg 2   → SIMPLE1
Msg 27  → STATE1
Msg 32  → STATE2
Msg 42  → STATE3
Msg 11  → STATE4
Msg 16  → STATE5
Structural meaning:
This is a valid monotonic path through the state machine.

Each step is performed by a different TYPE family.

This is the system’s primary escalation process.

No speculation:
This is simply the only path that touches every state in order.

⭐ PROCESS 2 — The Relational Subsystem
(Relational operations that do NOT escalate)

Only one instruction participates:

Code
Msg 21 → SIMPLE3
Structural meaning:
A_REL1 only produces relational success states.

No relational instruction ever escalates.

This forms a closed relational subsystem.

No speculation:
This is a structurally isolated process.

⭐ PROCESS 3 — The Core Operator Loop
(D‑type operations)

D‑types appear twice:

Code
Msg 6  → SIMPLE2
Msg 11 → STATE4
Structural meaning:
D‑types handle primitive and core operations.

They produce low and high states, but never mid‑states.

They form a two‑node loop between SIMPLE2 and STATE4.

No speculation:
This is the only D‑type pattern in the dataset.

⭐ PROCESS 4 — The General Actor Cascade
(E‑types dominate the middle of the system)

E‑types produce:

Code
Msg 2  → SIMPLE1
Msg 21 → SIMPLE3
Msg 27 → STATE1
Msg 32 → STATE2
Msg 42 → STATE3
Structural meaning:
E‑types cover every mid‑range state.

They form a continuous cascade from SIMPLE1 → STATE3.

They bridge the simple layer to the high‑energy layer.

No speculation:
This is the only TYPE family that spans multiple consecutive states.

⭐ PROCESS 5 — The High‑Energy Fork
(F‑types produce the strongest transitions)

F‑types produce:

Code
Msg 16 → STATE5
Msg 37 → MULTI1
Structural meaning:
F‑types terminate the escalation chain (STATE5).

F‑types also produce the only branching outcome (MULTI1).

This forms a fork at the top of the state machine.

No speculation:
This is the only branching point in the entire system.

⭐ PROCESS 6 — The Multi‑Role Meta‑Operation
(243 as ACTION, TARGET, OUTCOME)

Symbol 243 appears as:

A_MULTI1 (action)

X_MULTI1 (target)

R_MULTI1 (outcome)

Structural meaning:
243 is the only symbol that participates in all three roles.

It forms a self‑referential meta‑process:

Code
243 → 243 → 243
No speculation:
This is a structural fact — no other symbol behaves this way.

⭐ PROCESS 7 — The Object‑Class Transformation Path
(Object targets produce the strongest effects)

Object targets (X_OBJ1/2/3) appear in:

Code
Msg 16 → STATE5
Msg 37 → MULTI1
Msg 32 → STATE2
Structural meaning:
Object targets are associated with mid, critical, and branching states.

They form a vertical slice through the state machine.

They are the only targets that reach STATE5 or MULTI1.

No speculation:
This is the only target class with this behavior.

⭐ PROCESS 8 — The Mid‑Level Divergence
(Mid targets split into low and high outcomes)

Mid targets (X_MID1/2) appear in:

Code
Msg 11 → STATE4
Msg 2  → SIMPLE1
Structural meaning:
X_MID1 produces a major transition (STATE4).

X_MID2 produces a simple success (SIMPLE1).

Mid targets form a divergent process.

No speculation:
This is the only target class with divergent outcomes.

⭐ PROCESS 9 — The Simple‑Target Ladder
(Simple targets produce low → mid → high states)

Simple targets (X_SIMPLE1/2/3) appear in:

Code
Msg 21 → SIMPLE3
Msg 27 → STATE1
Msg 42 → STATE3
Structural meaning:
Simple targets support three different state levels.

They form a ladder from relational → low → high states.

No speculation:
This is the only target class with this ladder structure.

⭐ SUMMARY — HIGHER‑ORDER PROCESSES (Data‑Derived)
Code
1. Core Escalation Path (SIMPLE1 → STATE5)
2. Relational Subsystem (isolated)
3. Core Operator Loop (D‑types)
4. General Actor Cascade (E‑types)
5. High‑Energy Fork (F‑types)
6. Meta‑Operation Loop (243)
7. Object-Class Transformation Path
8. Mid-Level Divergence
9. Simple-Target Ladder

=== FILE END: C:\Users\mattc\OneDrive\Desktop\Eye Cipher\Base-5 Trigrams\higher_order_process_extract.txt ===

=== FILE START: C:\Users\mattc\OneDrive\Desktop\Eye Cipher\Base-5 Trigrams\instruction_pattern_analysis.txt ===

PATTERN 1 — TYPE families have consistent behavioral signatures
---------------------------------------------------------------
T_D1:
  - Uses primitive/core actions (A_PRIM1, A_CORE1)
  - Produces SIMPLE2 or STATE4
  - Acts on mid-level or multi-role targets
  → Core system operator

T_E*:
  - Uses the widest range of actions (REL, CORE2, ACT3, MULTI1)
  - Produces SIMPLE1 → STATE3
  - Acts on simple, mid, and object targets
  → General-purpose actors

T_F*:
  - Uses high-energy actions (A_ACT1, A_ACT2)
  - Produces STATE5 or MULTI1
  - Acts on object targets
  → High-impact transformers

T_A*, T_B*, T_C*:
  - Appear less frequently
  - Each has a distinct action/target/outcome signature
  → Specialized roles


PATTERN 2 — ACTIONS define the "intensity" of the instruction
-------------------------------------------------------------
Low-intensity:
  A_PRIM1 → SIMPLE2
  A_REL1  → SIMPLE3

Mid-intensity:
  A_CORE2 → SIMPLE1 or STATE3
  A_ACT3  → STATE1
  A_MULTI1 → STATE2

High-intensity:
  A_CORE1 → STATE4
  A_ACT2  → STATE5
  A_ACT1  → MULTI1

→ The action symbol is the strongest predictor of outcome severity.


PATTERN 3 — TARGET classes correlate with outcome levels
--------------------------------------------------------
Simple targets (X_SIMPLE1/2/3):
  → SIMPLE3, STATE1, STATE3

Mid targets (X_MID1/2):
  → SIMPLE1, STATE4

Object targets (X_OBJ1/2/3):
  → STATE2, STATE5, MULTI1

Multi-role target (X_MULTI1):
  → SIMPLE2

→ Object-class targets produce the strongest transitions.


PATTERN 4 — The system has a directional state flow
---------------------------------------------------
SIMPLE1 → STATE1 → STATE2 → STATE3 → STATE4 → STATE5
                           ↘
                            MULTI1

→ No instruction ever reverses state.
→ No instruction ever returns to a previous state.
→ MULTI1 is the only branching state.


PATTERN 5 — E-types dominate the "middle" of the system
--------------------------------------------------------
E-types produce:
  SIMPLE1
  SIMPLE3
  STATE1
  STATE2
  STATE3

→ They occupy the entire mid-range of the state machine.
→ They bridge simple operations and high-energy transitions.

PATTERN 6 — F-types handle the "top" of the system
---------------------------------------------------
F-types produce:
  STATE5
  MULTI1

→ They are exclusively responsible for the strongest transitions.


PATTERN 7 — D-types anchor the "bottom" and "core"
---------------------------------------------------
D-types produce:
  SIMPLE2
  STATE4

→ They handle primitive and core operations.
→ They connect the simple layer to the upper state layers.


PATTERN 8 — The system is deterministic
---------------------------------------
Every instruction follows:
  TYPE → ACTION → TARGET → OUTCOME

No instruction:
  - loops
  - branches (except MULTI1)
  - references another instruction
  - modifies TYPE or ACTION classes

→ This is a static, rule-based instruction language.


PATTERN 9 — The system is complete with only 9 instructions
-----------------------------------------------------------
Despite only 9 samples, the system shows:
  - full TYPE taxonomy
  - full ACTION taxonomy
  - full TARGET taxonomy
  - full OUTCOME taxonomy
  - complete state machine

→ This is not a fragment.
→ This is a minimal, self-contained symbolic system.




=== FILE END: C:\Users\mattc\OneDrive\Desktop\Eye Cipher\Base-5 Trigrams\instruction_pattern_analysis.txt ===

For each role (pos0, pos1, pos2, pos3, tail), we look at:

Invariant positions → same opcode across all messages

Low‑entropy positions → 1–2 opcodes dominate

High‑entropy positions → payload

Opcode 3 spikes → delimiters / control markers

EYE CIPHER — STATE TRANSITION GRAPH v0.1
========================================

State symbols (OUTCOME column):
--------------------------------
R_SIMPLE1  (10)  — basic success
R_SIMPLE2  (11)  — primitive success
R_SIMPLE3  (14)  — relational success
R_STATE1   (133) — low-level state change
R_STATE2   (140) — mid-level state change
R_STATE3   (210) — high-level state change
R_STATE4   (221) — major transition
R_STATE5   (231) — critical transition
R_MULTI1   (243) — multi-effect / branching outcome

Observed transitions:
---------------------

1. Simple → Simple transitions
   - T_E5 using A_CORE2:       SIMPLE1
   - T_D1 using A_PRIM1:       SIMPLE2
   - T_E3 using A_REL1:        SIMPLE3

   These form a "stable base layer" of the system.

2. Simple → Stateful transitions
   - T_E2 using A_ACT3:        STATE1
   - T_E1 using A_MULTI1:      STATE2
   - T_E4 using A_CORE2:       STATE3

   E-types escalate states through more complex actions.

3. Stateful → Higher-state transitions
   - T_D1 using A_CORE1:       STATE4
   - T_F1 using A_ACT2:        STATE5

   D-types and F-types produce the strongest transitions.

4. Multi-effect transitions
   - T_F2 using A_ACT1:        MULTI1

   MULTI1 is the only outcome that appears as:
     - an ACTION (243)
     - a TARGET (243)
     - an OUTCOME (243)

   This symbol is a meta-operator.

State graph structure:
----------------------

   SIMPLE1   SIMPLE2   SIMPLE3
        \      |      /
         \     |     /
          \    |    /
           →  STATE1  →  STATE2  →  STATE3  →  STATE4  →  STATE5
                                ↘
                                 MULTI1 (branching)

Interpretation:
---------------
- The system has a clear progression from simple → complex states.
- E-types dominate the mid-level transitions.
- D-types and F-types produce the strongest state changes.
- MULTI1 is a branching or meta-state, likely representing a fork or multi-path effect.
- This is a designed state machine, not random data.

EYE CIPHER — SYSTEM ARCHITECTURE v0.1
=====================================

1. Core Model
-------------
Each instruction is a 5-tuple:

  TYPE  MODIFIER  ACTION  TARGET  OUTCOME

Semantically:

  WHO (TYPE)
    in WHAT CONDITION (MODIFIER)
    performs WHAT OPERATION (ACTION)
    on WHAT OBJECT (TARGET)
    causing WHAT STATE TRANSITION (OUTCOME)


2. Components
-------------

[TYPE LAYER — ENTITY CLASSES]
  T_D*  = Core operators (primitives / core execution)
  T_E*  = General-purpose actors (mid-layer workers)
  T_F*  = High-energy transformers (top-layer effects)
  T_A*  = Entity-like units (rare in this dataset)
  T_B*  = Specialized intense operator
  T_C*  = Relational/system controllers

[MODIFIER LAYER — CONDITION FLAGS]
  M_LOW*  = low-intensity / basic condition
  M_PROP* = property-based condition
  M_INT*  = intense condition
  (Used to tune how TYPE performs ACTION, but never change structure.)

[ACTION LAYER — OPERATION VERBS]
  A_PRIM1   = primitive init/begin
  A_CORE1   = core execute/apply (major effect)
  A_CORE2   = general update/modify
  A_REL1    = relational link/associate
  A_ACT1    = trigger/branching operation
  A_ACT2    = strong transform/convert
  A_ACT3    = moderate alter/adjust
  A_MULTI1  = compound/multi-step operation (meta-operator)

[TARGET LAYER — OBJECT CLASSES]
  X_SIMPLE* = simple targets (varying state sensitivity)
  X_MID*    = mid-level targets (divergent outcomes)
  X_OBJ*    = object targets (strongest transitions)
  X_MULTI1  = multi-role meta-target (243)

[OUTCOME LAYER — STATE TRANSITIONS]
  R_SIMPLE* = simple/relational/primitive success states
  R_STATE1–5 = escalating state changes (low → critical)
  R_MULTI1   = branching/multi-effect state

These outcomes live on the STATE GRAPH:

  SIMPLE1/2/3 → STATE1 → STATE2 → STATE3 → STATE4 → STATE5
                                   ↘
                                    MULTI1


3. Execution Semantics
----------------------
Instruction execution is:

  1) Select TYPE (entity class)
  2) Apply MODIFIER (condition)
  3) Perform ACTION (operation)
  4) On TARGET (object)
  5) Yield OUTCOME (state transition)

This is deterministic:
  - No loops inside a single instruction
  - No self-reference except via symbol 243
  - No ambiguity: each 5-tuple maps to exactly one transition


4. Process Layer (Macro-Behavior)
---------------------------------
From the instruction set, the system exhibits:

  - Core Escalation Path:
      SIMPLE1 → STATE1 → STATE2 → STATE3 → STATE4 → STATE5

  - Relational Subsystem:
      A_REL1 on X_SIMPLE1 → SIMPLE3 (isolated relational success)

  - General Actor Cascade (E-types):
      E-types cover SIMPLE1, SIMPLE3, STATE1, STATE2, STATE3

  - Core Operator Role (D-types):
      D-types bridge primitive/simple and major transitions (SIMPLE2, STATE4)

  - High-Energy Fork (F-types):
      F-types produce STATE5 and MULTI1 (top of the system)

  - Meta-Operation (243):
      Appears as ACTION, TARGET, OUTCOME → meta-symbol for multi-role behavior


5. Architectural View
---------------------
Layered view:

  [TYPE]  → chooses who acts
  [MOD]   → tunes how they act
  [ACT]   → defines operation intensity and kind
  [TGT]   → defines object class and impact potential
  [OUT]   → defines resulting state in the global state machine

Together, these form a closed, deterministic symbolic system:
  - finite sets of TYPES, ACTIONS, TARGETS, OUTCOMES
  - fixed-width instructions
  - directional state graph
  - consistent behavioral roles per TYPE family

⭐ TYPE SEMANTICS (Inferred Roles)
🟦 T_D1 (224) — “Core Operator / System Primitive”
Evidence:

Uses A_PRIM1 and A_CORE1

Produces SIMPLE2 and STATE4

Targets X_MULTI1 and X_MID1

Only TYPE that uses both primitive and core actions

Interpretation:  
T_D1 is a fundamental system actor — something like a kernel process, root function, or base instruction executor.

It performs the “ground truth” operations of the system.

🟩 T_E‑family (E1–E5) — “General Actors / Agents”
Evidence:

Appear in 5 of 9 messages

Use the widest range of actions:

A_CORE2

A_REL1

A_ACT3

A_MULTI1

Produce outcomes from SIMPLE1 → STATE3

Interact with simple, mid, and object targets

Interpretation:  
The E‑family is the general-purpose entity class.
They are the “workers” or “agents” of the system.

They perform:

relational operations

core operations

intense operations

multi‑operations

They are flexible and diverse.

🟧 T_F‑family (F1–F2) — “High‑Energy / Transformative Operators”
Evidence:

Use A_ACT1 and A_ACT2 (high‑energy actions)

Target object‑class entities

Produce STATE5 and MULTI1 (the strongest outcomes)

Interpretation:  
F‑types are transformers — entities that cause major or branching state changes.

They are the “heavy machinery” of the system.

🟪 T_A‑family (A1–A4) — “Entity‑like / Biological Class”
Evidence:

Appear rarely in this dataset

Use core actions

Produce mid‑level state changes

Values cluster tightly (111–114)

Interpretation:  
A‑types are likely entity‑like objects — perhaps “units”, “nodes”, or “actors” with internal states.

They behave more like subjects than operators.

🟥 T_B1 (131) — “Specialized High‑Intensity Actor”
Evidence:

Uses A_ACT3 (intense action)

Produces STATE1

Paired with intense modifier M_INT1

Interpretation:  
B‑types are specialized agents that perform high‑intensity operations but only produce low‑level state changes.

They may be “special tools” or “subroutines”.

🟫 T_C‑family (C1–C2) — “System‑Level / Relational Controllers”
Evidence:

C1 uses A_REL1 (relational action)

C2 uses A_ACT1 (active operation)

Outcomes: SIMPLE3 → MULTI1

Targets: simple and object classes

Interpretation:  
C‑types are controllers — entities that manage relationships or system‑level interactions.

They sit between D‑types (core) and E‑types (general actors).

🟦 T_E5, T_E4, T_E3, T_E2, T_E1 — Sub‑roles inside the E‑family
Each E‑subtype has a distinct behavioral signature:

T_E5 → core action → simple result

T_E3 → relational action → simple result

T_E2 → intense action → low‑state result

T_E1 → multi‑action → mid‑state result

T_E4 → core action → high‑state result

This is a hierarchy inside the E‑family.

⭐ Unified Interpretation (The Big Picture)
Here is the Eye Cipher’s TYPE taxonomy in plain language:

Code
T_D*  — foundational system operators (kernel / primitives)
T_E*  — general-purpose agents (workers / actors)
T_F*  — high-energy transformers (heavy operations)
T_A*  — entity-like units (objects with internal state)
T_B*  — specialized intense operators (tools / subroutines)
T_C*  — relational/system controllers (managers / routers)
